<div>PMCID: PMC12657424  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41223785/" class="usa-link">41223785</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abs0010"><h2>Summary</h2>
<section id="sec1_pmc"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Background</h3>
<p>Saliva is an emerging but understudied biofluid in metabolomics, offering a similar window into biological information as blood, while enabling repetitive and non-invasive sampling.</p></section><section id="sec2_pmc"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h3>
<p>In this observational study, we comprehensively analysed the salivary metabolome of 965 children and adolescents (7–17y) from two exploratory cohorts in Europe, using ultra-high performance liquid chromatography coupled to high-resolution mass spectrometry. Preprocessed data were assessed via (partial) Spearman correlation, Mummichog-based pathway enrichment, and univariate (Welsh t-test, one-way ANOVA, Wilcoxon or Kruskal–Wallis rank-sum test) analyses. All <em>p</em>-values were corrected using the Benjamini-Hochberg procedure. Associations with age, anthropometrics, mental wellbeing, lifestyle, dietary intake and microbiome were explored. Key metabolite associations were validated in two independent cohorts (8–18y, n = 471), yielding a total study population of 1436 participants.</p></section><section id="sec3_pmc"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Findings</h3>
<p>Over 3500 untargeted features and 188 targeted metabolites were consistently detected in the pooled quality control saliva of the exploratory cohorts. Weight status (|ρ| &lt; 0.255) and age (|ρ| &lt; 0.341) reflected the salivary metabolome best, compared to mental wellbeing (|ρ| &lt; 0.227) and lifestyle factors (|ρ| &lt; 0.185). Multiple metabolites, especially (acetylated) amino acids and derivatives were elevated in participants with overweight or obesity and those experiencing high psychological stress, mirroring blood-based studies. In addition, these metabolites correlated with Bacteroidota, Pseudomonadota, Actinomycetota and Bacillota (|ρ| &lt; 0.423) phyla, suggesting oral microbiome disturbances. Notably, the artificial sweetener acesulfame K, detected for the first time in children and adolescents’ saliva, was associated with excess weight (|ρ| &lt; 0.243), psychological stress (|ρ| &lt; 0.148), specific food and/or beverage consumption (|ρ| &lt; 0.348) and <em>Bacillota</em> spp. (|ρ| &lt; 0.196).</p></section><section id="sec4_pmc"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Interpretation</h3>
<p>Our findings reinforce and expand upon previous blood-based studies, while revealing novel salivary biomarkers and, as such, provide a high-quality repository of the paediatric salivary metabolome for future studies exploring its association with children and adolescent's health.</p></section><section id="sec5_pmc"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Funding</h3>
<p>FAME and OPERA were supported by the Research Foundation Flanders, Ghent University, and the European Research Council. PANIC was funded by Finnish national agencies and foundations. Fin-HIT was funded by the Folkhälsan Research Foundation and the Päivikki and Sakari Sohlberg Foundation.</p></section><section id="kwrds0010" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Metabolomics, Paediatric health, Saliva, Obesity</p></section></section><hr class="headless">
<section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="dtbox1"><h2 class="obj_head">Research in context.</h2>
<section id="dtbox1sec1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Evidence before this study</h3>
<p id="p0015">Previous research has shown that the metabolome, influenced by genetics and external factors such as diet, lifestyle, and microbiome, is crucial for understanding complex diseases like obesity and depression. Saliva, as a non-invasive biofluid, offers a practical alternative to blood for metabolomics studies, especially in children and adolescents. However, past studies have faced reproducibility issues due to small sample sizes and non-optimised analytical methods. No comprehensive studies have yet explored and externally validated the key drivers of the salivary metabolome in relation to health in children and adolescents.</p></section><section id="dtbox1sec2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Added value of this study</h3>
<p id="p0020">This study provides a large-scale, comprehensive analysis of the salivary metabolome in children and adolescents, revealing both known – (acetylated) amino acids and derivatives – and novel biomarkers – acesulfame K – associated with weight status and mental wellbeing. These findings were validated in independent cohorts, enhancing the robustness and applicability of saliva for paediatric health monitoring.</p></section><section id="dtbox1sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Implications of all the available evidence</h3>
<p id="p0025">The results of this study demonstrate that saliva is a valuable non-invasive biofluid for monitoring paediatric health. This research provides new insights into the metabolic profiles associated with age, anthropometrics, mental wellbeing, and lifestyle factors, and how these profiles are related to dietary habits and microbial strains. By establishing a comprehensive salivary repository, our study enables future research to explore children and adolescents' health in greater depth. This repository paves the way for early diagnosis and personalised interventions, leveraging the salivary metabolome's association with health outcomes to improve paediatric medicine.</p></section></section><section id="sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="p0030">The metabolome represents a functional quantifiable readout of the human phenotype, reflecting metabolic activities within different cells, tissues, organs, and body fluids.<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1"><sup>1</sup></a> As a result, it is unique and dynamic, driven by an individual's genome and exposome. The latter constitutes all external drivers an individual is exposed to, comprising mainly dietary, drug and supplement intake, psychosocial environment, lifestyle including physical activity (PA) and sleep, and the gastrointestinal microbiome.<a href="#bib2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib2"><sup>2</sup></a> Unravelling the extent to which these various drivers affect the human metabolome is crucial for understanding the molecular origins of and the (patho)physiological pathways in which relevant metabolites can be mapped. This should ultimately enable correlating a particular metabotype to phenotypes of health and disease.<a href="#bib3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib3">3</a>, <a href="#bib4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib4">4</a>, <a href="#bib5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib5">5</a> Since metabolic alterations can be observed long before clinical manifestations, metabolomics facilitates both the elucidation of early-onset metabolic alterations and the prediction of disease susceptibility and prognosis.<a href="#bib6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib6"><sup>6</sup></a></p>
<p id="p0035">In recent years, metabolomics has contributed to the understanding of multiple complex diseases including obesity,<a href="#bib5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib5"><sup>5</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7"><sup>7</sup></a> depression,<a href="#bib8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib8"><sup>8</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib9"><sup>9</sup></a> type 2 diabetes,<a href="#bib10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib10"><sup>10</sup></a> cardiovascular disease<a href="#bib11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib11"><sup>11</sup></a> and multiple cancers.<a href="#bib12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib12"><sup>12</sup></a> These and other non-communicable diseases (NCDs) account for 43 million deaths annually, representing 75% of all deaths worldwide.<a href="#bib13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib13"><sup>13</sup></a> It is worth pointing out that several NCDs often arise in early childhood, when healthy growth and development are crucial.<a href="#bib6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib6"><sup>6</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7"><sup>7</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib14"><sup>14</sup></a> Specifically, more than 400 million children and adolescents are currently suffering from overweight or obesity.<a href="#bib15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib15"><sup>15</sup></a> Increasing levels of obesity are associated with reduced health-related quality of life,<a href="#bib16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib16"><sup>16</sup></a> while posing a risk factor for the development of other NCDs and ultimately to premature death in adulthood.<a href="#bib17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib17"><sup>17</sup></a> Therefore, it is particularly important to study the metabolome of children and adolescents and its main determinants, taking into account exposures in early life that may trigger the biological mechanism of diseases.<a href="#bib18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib18"><sup>18</sup></a> This will not only allow for early diagnosis and prognosis but also pave the way towards the design of personalised intervention strategies promoting a healthy metabolome.<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1"><sup>1</sup></a></p>
<p id="p0040">The results of metabolomics studies have proven difficult to reproduce due to the personalised nature of the metabolome<a href="#bib1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib1"><sup>1</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7"><sup>7</sup></a> and small cohort sizes,<a href="#bib19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib19">19</a>, <a href="#bib20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib20">20</a>, <a href="#bib21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib21">21</a>, <a href="#bib22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib22">22</a>, <a href="#bib23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib23">23</a> highlighting the need for large cohort studies. Although urine has demonstrated its potential in studying different NCDs,<a href="#bib7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib7"><sup>7</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib24"><sup>24</sup></a> there is some inconvenience with on-site and repeated sampling. For both clinical and metabolomics applications, blood plasma and serum are popular because they reflect systemic changes well.<a href="#bib25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib25"><sup>25</sup></a> In vulnerable populations such as children, however, blood sampling is less feasible because it is considered painful, stressful and requires trained personnel. In this context, saliva is a valuable blood surrogate.<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26"><sup>26</sup></a> Its painless, inexpensive and non-invasive nature of sample collection increases readiness and ensures compliance with large-scale and long-term studies.<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26"><sup>26</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27"><sup>27</sup></a> Since the oral cavity is host to both microbial and viral cells, the salivary metabolome comprises exogenous and endogenous metabolic products, the latter by reflecting circulating metabolite levels via passive diffusion from blood.<a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27"><sup>27</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib28"><sup>28</sup></a> Saliva thus presents superior properties to blood, as it provides a mirror of the interactions between the microbiome, diet and host metabolome.<a href="#bib26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib26"><sup>26</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27"><sup>27</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib29"><sup>29</sup></a> To the best of our knowledge, for the salivary metabolome, no studies report on its key drivers in relation to health yet.</p>
<p id="p0045">In this cross-sectional study, we are the first to comprehensively characterize the salivary metabolome of children and adolescents, analysing samples from 1436 participants across four independent European paediatric cohorts (two from Belgium, two from Finland). Our main objectives were to unravel the extent to which the salivary metabolome reflects age and anthropometric parameters, particularly weight status, as well as indicators of mental wellbeing and lifestyle, and how food consumption and microbial composition contribute to that.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Paediatric cohorts</h3>
<p id="p0050">An overview of the different cohorts and collected data can be consulted in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>. The FAME (Flemish Adolescent MEtabolome)<a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30"><sup>31</sup></a> and Fin-HIT (Finnish Health In Teens)<a href="#bib31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib31"><sup>32</sup></a> cohorts served as exploratory cohorts, while data from the PANIC (Physical Activity and Nutrition in Children)<a href="#bib32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib32"><sup>33</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib33"><sup>34</sup></a> and OPERA (Obesity Prevention through Emotion Regulation in Adolescents)<a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34"><sup>35</sup></a> cohorts were utilised for independent validation.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">Fig. 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12657424_gr1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/eea0/12657424/4ce5d85dc60a/gr1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="673" width="747" alt="Fig. 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Schematic study design. Four different cohorts totalling 1436 children or adolescents from whom one saliva sample was obtained for metabolomics analyses according to Wijnant et al.<a href="#bib27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib27"><sup>27</sup></a> Where marked, age (mean ± SD), sex (F = female), weight category, mental wellbeing, lifestyle (including sleep duration, screen time and physical activity), dietary habits and salivary microbiome data were collected. Classification of overweight and obesity was based on Cole et al.<a href="#bib44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib44"><sup>30</sup></a></p></figcaption></figure><p id="p0055">Briefly, the FAME<a href="#bib30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib30"><sup>31</sup></a> study (<a href="http://ClinicalTrials.gov" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">ClinicalTrials.gov</a> <a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT06391671" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">NCT06391671</a>) was conducted in both school and obesity clinic settings, with cross-sectional data collection taking place between February 2022 and May 2023. Anthropometric measurements, including height, weight, waist circumference, and body fat percentage (BF%), were taken using standardised methods by a trained professional, and BMI was calculated and adjusted for age and sex according to Belgian reference standards (BMI<em>z</em>).<a href="#bib35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib35"><sup>36</sup></a> Personal data were collected alongside information on mental wellbeing and food intake through online self-report questionnaires. Stress perception was assessed using the Perceived Stress Scale (PSS), a 10-item instrument to evaluate the degree to which an individual has perceived life as unpredictable, uncontrollable and overloading over the previous month.<a href="#bib36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib36"><sup>37</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37"><sup>38</sup></a> Depressive symptoms were monitored with the Children's Depression Inventory (CDI-2), a 28-item questionnaire that is a validated instrument for measuring cognitive, affective, and behavioural indicators of depression in children and adolescents aged 8–21 years.<a href="#bib38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib38"><sup>39</sup></a> Self-esteem was measured using the Rosenberg Self-Esteem Scale (RSES), a validated 10-item questionnaire to assess participants' self-esteem and perceived self-worth.<a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39"><sup>40</sup></a> Eating behaviour (external, restrained and emotional eating) was recorded using the 33-item Dutch Eating Behaviour Questionnaire (DEBQ),<a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40"><sup>41</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41"><sup>42</sup></a> while dietary patterns were assessed with a 59-item food frequency questionnaire (FFQ) adapted from the Children's Eating Habits Questionnaire, designed to assess food consumption frequencies associated with overweight, obesity, and general health in the IDEFICS project.<a href="#bib42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib42"><sup>43</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib43"><sup>44</sup></a> At least one guardian and the child jointly reported the frequency of the participant's consumption of selected food items during a typical week in the preceding 4 weeks, using the following response options: ‘(almost) never’, ‘a few times a week’, ‘(almost) daily’, or ‘multiple times per day’. These frequency categories were subsequently assigned numerical values of 0, 1, 2, and 3, respectively, without portion size quantification (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>).</p>
<p id="p0060">The Fin-HIT<a href="#bib31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib31"><sup>32</sup></a> study was conducted between 2011 and 2014, primarily in a school setting. Trained fieldworkers measured the participants’ weight, height and waist circumference using standardised methods. BMI scores were calculated and adjusted using the age- and sex-specific reference values of the International Obesity Task Force criteria (IOTF) to obtain the BMI<em>z</em>.<a href="#bib44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib44"><sup>30</sup></a> Self-esteem was measured using the RSES,<a href="#bib39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib39"><sup>40</sup></a> and body image (BI) was assessed using the Collins Childhood Body Rating Scales, a pictorial scale consisting of seven sex-specific images, representing a range of body sizes from very thin (1) to obese (7).<a href="#bib45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib45"><sup>45</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib46"><sup>46</sup></a> Participants were asked to select the image that most resembled their current body shape and the one that reflected their desired body shape. The discrepancy between the current and desired body shape was then calculated as an indicator of body dissatisfaction. PA was assessed by self-reporting of weekly hours of leisure-time exercise, while sleep habits were reported separately for school and non-school days, including waking and bedtime hours to calculate sleep durations during weekdays and weekends separately. Screen time was evaluated using questions adapted from the WHO Health Behaviour in School-aged Children (HBSC) study.<a href="#bib47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib47"><sup>47</sup></a> Dietary intake was evaluated using an FFQ, adapted from the HBSC study, that captured the consumption frequencies of 16 food and beverage items.<a href="#bib47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib47"><sup>47</sup></a> Responses were recorded on a seven-point scale ranging from 0 (not consumed) to 6 (consumed several times per day), indicating the frequency of consumption for each item during the past month (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Table S2</a>).</p>
<p id="p0065">The PANIC study<a href="#bib32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib32"><sup>33</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib33"><sup>34</sup></a> (<a href="http://ClinicalTrials.gov" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">ClinicalTrials.gov</a> <a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT01803776" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">NCT01803776</a>) is being conducted at the Institute of Biomedicine, University of Eastern Finland, Kuopio Campus, Finland. Baseline data collection occurred between 2007 and 2009. Trained research staff collected anthropometric data, including BF%, height, weight and waist circumference, with BMI scores calculated and adjusted for age and sex according to Finnish reference standards (BMI<em>z</em>).<a href="#bib48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib48"><sup>48</sup></a> Sleep duration was estimated using the Basic Nordic Sleep Questionnaire<a href="#bib49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib49"><sup>49</sup></a> adapted for guardians to complete. PA was assessed using the PANIC Physical Activity and Sedentary Behaviour Questionnaire, which measured total PA separately for five weekdays and two weekend days.<a href="#bib50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib50"><sup>50</sup></a> Total screen time was calculated by summing the use of different screens, such as TV, computer, game consoles, tablets, and mobile phones. Food consumption was measured using food records in grams per day, completed by parents over four consecutive days, consisting of two week and two weekend days, and was verified and analysed by clinical nutritionists using the Micro Nutrica® software (version 2.5, The Social Insurance Institution of Finland, Turku, Finland), incorporating food composition data from national analyses and international sources (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Table S3</a>).<a href="#bib51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib51"><sup>51</sup></a></p>
<p id="p0070">For the OPERA cohort<a href="#bib34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib34"><sup>35</sup></a> cross-sectional data were collected between March and August 2018. Anthropometric measures, including height, weight, waist circumference, and BF%, were collected by a study nurse, and BMI scores were adjusted for age and sex (BMI<em>z</em>).<a href="#bib35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib35"><sup>36</sup></a> Mental wellbeing was assessed using the PSS,<a href="#bib37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib37"><sup>38</sup></a> CDI-2,<a href="#bib38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib38"><sup>39</sup></a> the negative affect items of the Positive And Negative Affect Schedule for Children (PANAS-C)<a href="#bib52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib52"><sup>52</sup></a> and hair cortisol. The PANAS-C consists of 30 self-report questions, to measure the participant's emotions during the past few weeks. Participants were asked to rate the extent to which they experienced each feeling over the past few weeks, using a 5-point Likert-type scale ranging from ‘very slightly or not at all’ to ‘extremely’. Eating behaviour (emotional and external eating) and food consumption were evaluated, using respectively the DEBQ<a href="#bib40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib40"><sup>41</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib41"><sup>42</sup></a> and an FFQ<a href="#bib42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib42"><sup>43</sup></a><sup>,</sup><a href="#bib43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="bib43"><sup>44</sup></a> as in the FAME (<a href="#appsec1" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>). Lifestyle data were collected through questionnaires assessing screen time and PA.</p>
<p id="p0075">The included cohorts represent both population-based (PANIC, OPERA, Fin-HIT) and more dedicated (FAME) sampling strategies, enabling the investigation of salivary metabolite–phenotype associations across a broad spectrum of weight statuses. In Fin-HIT, a subset of participants with higher BMI<em>z</em> was selected, while FAME specifically oversampled participants with overweight and obesity. The percentage of missing metadata was generally low (&lt; 10% in FAME and OPERA, &lt; 1% in Fin-HIT, ≤ 5% PANIC, except FFQ which had &lt; 20% missingness) (<a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). Missing metadata were excluded from further analyses where applicable.</p>
